It’s like this jolt in my chest, like I’ve lost control of the whole situation. I can know, logically, that half a pound could be anything, but that doesn’t really matter in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’ve let it start. You’re putting weight back on.” And once that thought is there, it’s very hard to shake.\n\nThen I’ll spend the rest of the day cutting back harder. I’ll tell myself I need to correct it, tighten things up, not give myself any room to slide. It’s not just that I dislike the number. I can’t stand the idea of gaining weight back, of becoming less lean. It feels like something I have to stop immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, because “excessive” is a bit subjective, isn’t it? I mean, compared with what other people eat, I don’t really know. Compared with what I think is appropriate, almost anything beyond what I planned can feel like too much.\n\nBut if you mean losing control and eating a large amount very quickly, I can’t really think of a clear example like that. I don’t tend to sit down and eat a lot all at once. If anything, I’m usually very aware of what I’m having, maybe too aware. Sometimes even a small amount can feel like I’ve overdone it, but I don’t know that it would look “excessive” to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I’ve lost control while I’m eating, because I’m almost always paying attention to it—sometimes too closely. I’m aware of each bite, how much is left, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had enough. It’s not like I suddenly “come to” and realize I’ve eaten a lot without meaning to.\n\nThere are times where I feel unsettled if I eat more than I planned, or if something isn’t measured or structured the way I expected. Then I might feel like the situation is less controlled, if that makes sense. But that’s different from feeling unable to stop myself. It’s more that I feel anxious afterward, or like I need to mentally review what happened.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I lose control while eating. I tend to feel the opposite, really—like I’m trying very hard not to let anything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… I’m trying to think of what you’d count as “uncomfortably full,” because I don’t really like feeling full at all. Even feeling normally full can make me uneasy, if I notice it too much.\n\nBut eating to the point where I’m physically stuffed, where my stomach hurts or I feel like I can’t move comfortably—no, that’s not really something I do. I tend to stop well before that, or at least I try to. If I feel any heaviness afterward, I’ll focus on it, maybe more than I should, and it can feel worse in my head than it probably is physically.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I eat until I’m uncomfortably full. It’s more that I’m uncomfortable with fullness in general, even when it’s not actually excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know you’ve asked it a couple of different ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult, it’s just… I wouldn’t describe what happens with me as eating a large amount in a short period.\n\nIf I eat more than I expected, it can feel like a big deal to me, but when I step back and think about the actual amount, it’s usually not what most people would call large. I don’t have episodes where I’m rapidly eating a lot of food or feeling like I can’t stop. I’m usually very aware of what I’m eating while it’s happening.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not in that binge sort of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating in front of other people?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve pretty much stopped meeting people for meals unless I absolutely have to. At work, if the guys are going out after a shift or someone suggests grabbing lunch, I’ll usually find a reason not to go, or I’ll say I’ve already eaten, or I’ve got something to finish. It’s not that I don’t want to see them. I just don’t want to be sitting there with people looking at what I ordered, or asking why I’m not eating much, or making comments like they’re joking but they’re still noticing.\n\nAnd it’s not only social. It’s starting to bleed into work in a way I don’t like talking about. Occupational health has held up my clearance for field-related duties, and I can tell the team is… they’re second-guessing whether I’m fit to be out there. Nobody says it outright in those words, but you can feel it. In my line of work, if people start wondering if you can hold up, that matters.\n\nSo yes, I avoid eating in front of people because I don’t want the questions, the pressure, the attention. I’d rather just not put myself in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered normal before restricting your intake?\n\nPatient: Before I started cutting back like this? Yes, I mean… I used to think of a normal amount as just a regular meal. Nothing dramatic. Breakfast, lunch, dinner if the day allowed it, or at least something you could sit down and call a meal.\n\nNow that feels different. What brings me in, really, is occupational health saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose if I’m honest, most days I keep it pretty minimal. Black coffee gets me through a lot of the day. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on maybe a few bites of chicken at night. And in my head that feels controlled, like I’m doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nA full meal now feels… I don’t know, like I’m letting my standards slip. Like I’ve given in somehow. I know that probably sounds strange out loud, because a normal person would just call it dinner, but to me it feels like too much. So yes, I did used to consider more food normal before I started restricting it, but now my sense of “normal” has shifted pretty far.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight related to the idea that you would lose control or become less disciplined if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, although the number matters to me more than I like admitting. If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this immediate jolt, like something’s gone wrong, like I’ve slipped. I know half a pound can be nothing, logically—water, whatever—but in the moment it doesn’t feel like nothing. It feels like proof that I’m losing control.\n\nAnd then I spend the rest of the day trying to correct it. I’ll cut back harder, be stricter, watch every little thing, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow even a small gain, then I’m becoming less disciplined, like I’m letting myself go in some way. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds extreme, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a large amount, no. That’s the thing—I don’t really have episodes where I sit down and eat a huge amount quickly, at least not the way I imagine you mean it.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve eaten more than I planned, and it feels like a lot to me because my idea of “a lot” is probably not very normal right now. Like, if I had something extra or more than I’d decided I was going to have, I might feel uncomfortable about it afterward. But an actual period where I’m eating a large amount of food in a short time… I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because what feels large in my head might not look large to someone else. But no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in a way that felt out of control?\n\nPatient: Out of control… I don’t think so, not in the way I imagine you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel uncomfortable if I eat more than I had planned, and then my head starts going over it and over it, like, “Why did you do that?” But that’s not the same as feeling like I couldn’t stop myself. It’s more that I’m very aware of every bite, if anything. I’m usually watching it too closely.\n\nThere might be moments where I feel I’ve gone off plan, and emotionally that can feel like I’ve messed up, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating in a way that’s out of control. I don’t have that sense of being carried along and unable to stop. If anything, it’s the opposite—I’m trying to keep everything very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in secret because you were ashamed of what or how much you were eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. I mean, I’m ashamed of eating sometimes, or ashamed if I feel like I ate more than I should have, but I don’t really hide away and eat secretly in the sense of sneaking food or waiting until no one’s around so I can eat a lot.\n\nIf anything, I tend to avoid eating in front of people because I feel watched or judged, but that’s a little different from what you’re asking, I think. It’s not like I’m secretly eating because I’m embarrassed by the amount. I’m usually more embarrassed that I’m eating at all, or that I might be doing it “wrong,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of times where I was eating in secret because of what or how much I was eating. I might be private about food, but not in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was over a period of time that might seem normal to others?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to answer that honestly, because “excessive” is a word I use in my own head a lot, but I’m not sure it would match what you mean clinically.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I felt like what I ate was excessive, but if you wrote it down on paper, I don’t know that another person would see it that way. Like if I had more than I’d planned, or something that wasn’t part of what I’d already decided was acceptable, then in my mind it can become “too much.” Even if it happened over a normal meal, or just across an evening, it can feel excessive to me because I’m measuring it against this very strict idea I have of what I should be eating.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had times where I objectively ate a huge amount over a normal period and thought, “That was excessive” in the way you might be asking. It’s more that my reaction to it feels disproportionate. I’ll think about it afterward and feel like I’ve overdone it, even if part of me knows someone else might say it was just… a meal, or a snack, or not especially unusual.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve considered things excessive, yes, but I’m not sure they actually were excessive by anyone else’s standards.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were binge eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it binge eating, no. I mean, I’ve worried about that word before, because if I eat more than I intended, my mind can make it feel like some terrible episode, like I’ve crossed a line. But when I try to look at it plainly, it doesn’t feel like what people usually mean by bingeing.\n\nI haven’t had that feeling of… sitting down and eating a large amount and feeling unable to stop. It’s more that I might have something I didn’t plan for, or more than I thought I “should,” and then I get very stuck on it afterward. I can label it harshly in my head, like I’ll think, “That was too much,” or “You lost control,” but if I’m being accurate, I don’t think I actually was out of control in the eating itself.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a binge-eating episode. I’ve had times where I felt really distressed about eating, or felt like I’d overeaten by my own standards, but not a true binge, at least not as I understand it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating where you felt like you couldn't stop once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you mean. I’ve had times where once I’ve eaten something, my thoughts sort of spiral afterward, and I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “That was too much,” even if it wasn’t actually much. But during the eating itself, I don’t usually feel like I physically or mentally can’t stop.\n\nIt’s more like I’m very aware of every bite, almost too aware, so it’s not this automatic thing where I start and then suddenly I’ve eaten a lot and couldn’t control it. If anything, I’m usually trying to stay in control the whole time, maybe too much. I might feel upset afterward if I went past whatever rule I had in my head, but I don’t remember episodes where I felt like, “I can’t stop eating now that I’ve started.”\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. I get distressed about eating, and I can judge myself pretty harshly, but I don’t think I have that can’t-stop-once-I-start feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Honestly, not really. Or… I guess there have been times where I might look normal from the outside, like if I’m sitting with someone and I have a coffee or I pick at something, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal at all. It feels managed. Controlled. Like I’m calculating the whole thing.

Most days, if I’m being straight with you, I keep it very limited. Black coffee is easy because it feels clean, simple, like it doesn’t count in the same way. Maybe I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on at night a few bites of chicken, something basic. But a full meal… that’s hard for me. It feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m giving in to something I shouldn’t.

That’s actually part of why I’m here. Occupational health has been saying I’m not eating enough. I don’t always see it the same way in the moment, because to me it feels like discipline, or keeping myself where I need to be. But if you’re asking whether I have periods where I just eat normally without restricting it or thinking about it, no, I don’t think I really do. It’s almost always controlled in some way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I’m here because occupational health pushed the issue. They’ve been saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose, if I’m being precise, most days it’s just black coffee for me. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or at night maybe a few bites of chicken, nothing elaborate.\n\nI don’t really see it the way they do, though. To me, sitting down to a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m losing discipline. I’ve always held myself to a certain line, and once I start making exceptions, it feels like the whole thing gets away from me. So that’s what brings me in, more or less.\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it as simply as “I want to be thinner.” It’s more that when I look in the mirror, I don’t see what other people apparently see. They’ll say I look too lean or whatever, but that’s not what my eye goes to.\n\nI look at my stomach, my waist, the line of it. I’ll turn sideways, check whether there’s any softness there, and honestly it can set the tone for the whole day. If I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself, like I’m still in control. If I don’t, it bothers me. It feels like I’ve let myself slip, even if someone else would say that doesn’t make sense.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I want to be leaner, or at least not any less lean than I am. But it’s not vanity, exactly. It feels more like a standard I’m trying to hold.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds irrational, or weak, but yes, I am.\n\nIf the scale is up even half a pound, I feel it immediately. It’s like this jolt in my chest, like I’ve lost control of the whole situation. I can know, logically, that half a pound could be anything, but that doesn’t really matter in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’ve let it start. You’re putting weight back on.” And once that thought is there, it’s very hard to shake.\n\nThen I’ll spend the rest of the day cutting back harder. I’ll tell myself I need to correct it, tighten things up, not give myself any room to slide. It’s not just that I dislike the number. I can’t stand the idea of gaining weight back, of becoming less lean. It feels like something I have to stop immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, because “excessive” is a bit subjective, isn’t it? I mean, compared with what other people eat, I don’t really know. Compared with what I think is appropriate, almost anything beyond what I planned can feel like too much.\n\nBut if you mean losing control and eating a large amount very quickly, I can’t really think of a clear example like that. I don’t tend to sit down and eat a lot all at once. If anything, I’m usually very aware of what I’m having, maybe too aware. Sometimes even a small amount can feel like I’ve overdone it, but I don’t know that it would look “excessive” to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I’ve lost control while I’m eating, because I’m almost always paying attention to it—sometimes too closely. I’m aware of each bite, how much is left, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had enough. It’s not like I suddenly “come to” and realize I’ve eaten a lot without meaning to.\n\nThere are times where I feel unsettled if I eat more than I planned, or if something isn’t measured or structured the way I expected. Then I might feel like the situation is less controlled, if that makes sense. But that’s different from feeling unable to stop myself. It’s more that I feel anxious afterward, or like I need to mentally review what happened.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I lose control while eating. I tend to feel the opposite, really—like I’m trying very hard not to let anything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… I’m trying to think of what you’d count as “uncomfortably full,” because I don’t really like feeling full at all. Even feeling normally full can make me uneasy, if I notice it too much.\n\nBut eating to the point where I’m physically stuffed, where my stomach hurts or I feel like I can’t move comfortably—no, that’s not really something I do. I tend to stop well before that, or at least I try to. If I feel any heaviness afterward, I’ll focus on it, maybe more than I should, and it can feel worse in my head than it probably is physically.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I eat until I’m uncomfortably full. It’s more that I’m uncomfortable with fullness in general, even when it’s not actually excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know you’ve asked it a couple of different ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult, it’s just… I wouldn’t describe what happens with me as eating a large amount in a short period.\n\nIf I eat more than I expected, it can feel like a big deal to me, but when I step back and think about the actual amount, it’s usually not what most people would call large. I don’t have episodes where I’m rapidly eating a lot of food or feeling like I can’t stop. I’m usually very aware of what I’m eating while it’s happening.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not in that binge sort of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating in front of other people?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve pretty much stopped meeting people for meals unless I absolutely have to. At work, if the guys are going out after a shift or someone suggests grabbing lunch, I’ll usually find a reason not to go, or I’ll say I’ve already eaten, or I’ve got something to finish. It’s not that I don’t want to see them. I just don’t want to be sitting there with people looking at what I ordered, or asking why I’m not eating much, or making comments like they’re joking but they’re still noticing.\n\nAnd it’s not only social. It’s starting to bleed into work in a way I don’t like talking about. Occupational health has held up my clearance for field-related duties, and I can tell the team is… they’re second-guessing whether I’m fit to be out there. Nobody says it outright in those words, but you can feel it. In my line of work, if people start wondering if you can hold up, that matters.\n\nSo yes, I avoid eating in front of people because I don’t want the questions, the pressure, the attention. I’d rather just not put myself in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered normal before restricting your intake?\n\nPatient: Before I started cutting back like this? Yes, I mean… I used to think of a normal amount as just a regular meal. Nothing dramatic. Breakfast, lunch, dinner if the day allowed it, or at least something you could sit down and call a meal.\n\nNow that feels different. What brings me in, really, is occupational health saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose if I’m honest, most days I keep it pretty minimal. Black coffee gets me through a lot of the day. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on maybe a few bites of chicken at night. And in my head that feels controlled, like I’m doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nA full meal now feels… I don’t know, like I’m letting my standards slip. Like I’ve given in somehow. I know that probably sounds strange out loud, because a normal person would just call it dinner, but to me it feels like too much. So yes, I did used to consider more food normal before I started restricting it, but now my sense of “normal” has shifted pretty far.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight related to the idea that you would lose control or become less disciplined if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, although the number matters to me more than I like admitting. If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this immediate jolt, like something’s gone wrong, like I’ve slipped. I know half a pound can be nothing, logically—water, whatever—but in the moment it doesn’t feel like nothing. It feels like proof that I’m losing control.\n\nAnd then I spend the rest of the day trying to correct it. I’ll cut back harder, be stricter, watch every little thing, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow even a small gain, then I’m becoming less disciplined, like I’m letting myself go in some way. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds extreme, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a large amount, no. That’s the thing—I don’t really have episodes where I sit down and eat a huge amount quickly, at least not the way I imagine you mean it.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve eaten more than I planned, and it feels like a lot to me because my idea of “a lot” is probably not very normal right now. Like, if I had something extra or more than I’d decided I was going to have, I might feel uncomfortable about it afterward. But an actual period where I’m eating a large amount of food in a short time… I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because what feels large in my head might not look large to someone else. But no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in a way that felt out of control?\n\nPatient: Out of control… I don’t think so, not in the way I imagine you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel uncomfortable if I eat more than I had planned, and then my head starts going over it and over it, like, “Why did you do that?” But that’s not the same as feeling like I couldn’t stop myself. It’s more that I’m very aware of every bite, if anything. I’m usually watching it too closely.\n\nThere might be moments where I feel I’ve gone off plan, and emotionally that can feel like I’ve messed up, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating in a way that’s out of control. I don’t have that sense of being carried along and unable to stop. If anything, it’s the opposite—I’m trying to keep everything very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in secret because you were ashamed of what or how much you were eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. I mean, I’m ashamed of eating sometimes, or ashamed if I feel like I ate more than I should have, but I don’t really hide away and eat secretly in the sense of sneaking food or waiting until no one’s around so I can eat a lot.\n\nIf anything, I tend to avoid eating in front of people because I feel watched or judged, but that’s a little different from what you’re asking, I think. It’s not like I’m secretly eating because I’m embarrassed by the amount. I’m usually more embarrassed that I’m eating at all, or that I might be doing it “wrong,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of times where I was eating in secret because of what or how much I was eating. I might be private about food, but not in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was over a period of time that might seem normal to others?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to answer that honestly, because “excessive” is a word I use in my own head a lot, but I’m not sure it would match what you mean clinically.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I felt like what I ate was excessive, but if you wrote it down on paper, I don’t know that another person would see it that way. Like if I had more than I’d planned, or something that wasn’t part of what I’d already decided was acceptable, then in my mind it can become “too much.” Even if it happened over a normal meal, or just across an evening, it can feel excessive to me because I’m measuring it against this very strict idea I have of what I should be eating.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had times where I objectively ate a huge amount over a normal period and thought, “That was excessive” in the way you might be asking. It’s more that my reaction to it feels disproportionate. I’ll think about it afterward and feel like I’ve overdone it, even if part of me knows someone else might say it was just… a meal, or a snack, or not especially unusual.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve considered things excessive, yes, but I’m not sure they actually were excessive by anyone else’s standards.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were binge eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it binge eating, no. I mean, I’ve worried about that word before, because if I eat more than I intended, my mind can make it feel like some terrible episode, like I’ve crossed a line. But when I try to look at it plainly, it doesn’t feel like what people usually mean by bingeing.\n\nI haven’t had that feeling of… sitting down and eating a large amount and feeling unable to stop. It’s more that I might have something I didn’t plan for, or more than I thought I “should,” and then I get very stuck on it afterward. I can label it harshly in my head, like I’ll think, “That was too much,” or “You lost control,” but if I’m being accurate, I don’t think I actually was out of control in the eating itself.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a binge-eating episode. I’ve had times where I felt really distressed about eating, or felt like I’d overeaten by my own standards, but not a true binge, at least not as I understand it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating where you felt like you couldn't stop once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you mean. I’ve had times where once I’ve eaten something, my thoughts sort of spiral afterward, and I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “That was too much,” even if it wasn’t actually much. But during the eating itself, I don’t usually feel like I physically or mentally can’t stop.\n\nIt’s more like I’m very aware of every bite, almost too aware, so it’s not this automatic thing where I start and then suddenly I’ve eaten a lot and couldn’t control it. If anything, I’m usually trying to stay in control the whole time, maybe too much. I might feel upset afterward if I went past whatever rule I had in my head, but I don’t remember episodes where I felt like, “I can’t stop eating now that I’ve started.”\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. I get distressed about eating, and I can judge myself pretty harshly, but I don’t think I have that can’t-stop-once-I-start feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating normally, without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Or… I guess there have been times where I might look normal from the outside, like if I’m sitting with someone and I have a coffee or I pick at something, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal at all. It feels managed. Controlled. Like I’m calculating the whole thing.\n\nMost days, if I’m being straight with you, I keep it very limited. Black coffee is easy because it feels clean, simple, like it doesn’t count in the same way. Maybe I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on at night a few bites of chicken, something basic. But a full meal… that’s hard for me. It feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m giving in to something I shouldn’t.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I’m here. Occupational health has been saying I’m not eating enough. I don’t always see it the same way in the moment, because to me it feels like discipline, or keeping myself where I need to be. But if you’re asking whether I have periods where I just eat normally without restricting it or thinking about it, no, I don’t think I really do. It’s almost always controlled in some way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like if I had more than I’d planned, or something that wasn’t part of what I’d already decided was acceptable, then in my mind it can become “too much.” Even if it happened over a normal meal, or just across an evening, it can feel excessive to me because I’m measuring it against this very strict idea I have of what I should be eating.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had times where I objectively ate a huge amount over a normal period and thought, “That was excessive” in the way you might be asking. I’ll think about it afterward and feel like I’ve overdone it, even if part of me knows someone else might say it was just… a meal, or a snack, or not especially unusual.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve considered things excessive, yes, but I’m not sure they actually were excessive by anyone else’s standards.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were binge eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it binge eating, no. I mean, I’ve worried about that word before, because if I eat more than I intended, my mind can make it feel like some terrible episode, like I’ve crossed a line. But when I try to look at it plainly, it doesn’t feel like what people usually mean by bingeing.\n\nI haven’t had that feeling of… sitting down and eating a large amount and feeling unable to stop. It’s more that I might have something I didn’t plan for, or more than I thought I “should,” and then I get very stuck on it afterward. I can label it harshly in my head, like I’ll think, “That was too much,” or “You lost control,” but if I’m being accurate, I don’t think I actually was out of control in the eating itself.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a binge-eating episode. I’ve had times where I felt really distressed about eating, or felt like I’d overeaten by my own standards, but not a true binge, at least not as I understand it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating where you felt like you couldn't stop once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you mean. I’ve had times where once I’ve eaten something, my thoughts sort of spiral afterward, and I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “That was too much,” even if it wasn’t actually much. But during the eating itself, I don’t usually feel like I physically or mentally can’t stop.\n\nIt’s more like I’m very aware of every bite, almost too aware, so it’s not this automatic thing where I start and then suddenly I’ve eaten a lot and couldn’t control it. If anything, I’m usually trying to stay in control the whole time, maybe too much. I might feel upset afterward if I went past whatever rule I had in my head, but I don’t remember episodes where I felt like, “I can’t stop eating now that I’ve started.”\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. I get distressed about eating, and I can judge myself pretty harshly, but I don’t think I have that can’t-stop-once-I-start feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating normally, without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. It feels managed. Controlled. Like I’m calculating the whole thing.\n\nMost days, if I’m being straight with you, I keep it very limited. Black coffee is easy because it feels clean, simple, like it doesn’t count in the same way. Maybe I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on at night a few bites of chicken, something basic. But a full meal… that’s hard for me. It feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m giving in to something I shouldn’t.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I’m here. Occupational health has been saying I’m not eating enough. I don’t always see it the same way in the moment, because to me it feels like discipline, or keeping myself where I need to be. But if you’re asking whether I have periods where I just eat normally without restricting it or thinking about it, no, I don’t think I really do. It’s almost always controlled in some way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… both, I think, but if I’m honest, the body shape part is much bigger than I like admitting. I tell myself it’s about control, and it is, in a way. Controlling food feels like controlling the day, controlling whether I’ve done well or failed. But the reason it matters so much is tied to how I look.

When I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people say they see. If someone says I look thin, or that I don’t need to lose weight, it doesn’t land properly. I’ll still stand there and my eyes go straight to my stomach, my waist, that area. I’ll turn sideways, check whether it looks flat enough, lean enough. And then somehow that decides the whole day. If I think I look acceptable, I can respect myself a bit more. If I don’t, it’s like I’ve let myself go, even if nothing has actually changed.

So yes, the control is part of it, but it’s not just control in the abstract. It’s control aimed at keeping my body a certain way. Lean, contained, not soft. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s how it feels in my head. My food choices are very much driven by whether I think they’ll help me stay looking a certain way.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I’m here because occupational health pushed the issue. They’ve been saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose, if I’m being precise, most days it’s just black coffee for me. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or at night maybe a few bites of chicken, nothing elaborate.\n\nI don’t really see it the way they do, though. To me, sitting down to a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m losing discipline. I’ve always held myself to a certain line, and once I start making exceptions, it feels like the whole thing gets away from me. So that’s what brings me in, more or less.\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it as simply as “I want to be thinner.” It’s more that when I look in the mirror, I don’t see what other people apparently see. They’ll say I look too lean or whatever, but that’s not what my eye goes to.\n\nI look at my stomach, my waist, the line of it. I’ll turn sideways, check whether there’s any softness there, and honestly it can set the tone for the whole day. If I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself, like I’m still in control. If I don’t, it bothers me. It feels like I’ve let myself slip, even if someone else would say that doesn’t make sense.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I want to be leaner, or at least not any less lean than I am. But it’s not vanity, exactly. It feels more like a standard I’m trying to hold.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds irrational, or weak, but yes, I am.\n\nIf the scale is up even half a pound, I feel it immediately. It’s like this jolt in my chest, like I’ve lost control of the whole situation. I can know, logically, that half a pound could be anything, but that doesn’t really matter in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’ve let it start. You’re putting weight back on.” And once that thought is there, it’s very hard to shake.\n\nThen I’ll spend the rest of the day cutting back harder. I’ll tell myself I need to correct it, tighten things up, not give myself any room to slide. It’s not just that I dislike the number. I can’t stand the idea of gaining weight back, of becoming less lean. It feels like something I have to stop immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, because “excessive” is a bit subjective, isn’t it? I mean, compared with what other people eat, I don’t really know. Compared with what I think is appropriate, almost anything beyond what I planned can feel like too much.\n\nBut if you mean losing control and eating a large amount very quickly, I can’t really think of a clear example like that. I don’t tend to sit down and eat a lot all at once. If anything, I’m usually very aware of what I’m having, maybe too aware. Sometimes even a small amount can feel like I’ve overdone it, but I don’t know that it would look “excessive” to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I’ve lost control while I’m eating, because I’m almost always paying attention to it—sometimes too closely. I’m aware of each bite, how much is left, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had enough. It’s not like I suddenly “come to” and realize I’ve eaten a lot without meaning to.\n\nThere are times where I feel unsettled if I eat more than I planned, or if something isn’t measured or structured the way I expected. Then I might feel like the situation is less controlled, if that makes sense. But that’s different from feeling unable to stop myself. It’s more that I feel anxious afterward, or like I need to mentally review what happened.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I lose control while eating. I tend to feel the opposite, really—like I’m trying very hard not to let anything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… I’m trying to think of what you’d count as “uncomfortably full,” because I don’t really like feeling full at all. Even feeling normally full can make me uneasy, if I notice it too much.\n\nBut eating to the point where I’m physically stuffed, where my stomach hurts or I feel like I can’t move comfortably—no, that’s not really something I do. I tend to stop well before that, or at least I try to. If I feel any heaviness afterward, I’ll focus on it, maybe more than I should, and it can feel worse in my head than it probably is physically.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I eat until I’m uncomfortably full. It’s more that I’m uncomfortable with fullness in general, even when it’s not actually excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know you’ve asked it a couple of different ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult, it’s just… I wouldn’t describe what happens with me as eating a large amount in a short period.\n\nIf I eat more than I expected, it can feel like a big deal to me, but when I step back and think about the actual amount, it’s usually not what most people would call large. I don’t have episodes where I’m rapidly eating a lot of food or feeling like I can’t stop. I’m usually very aware of what I’m eating while it’s happening.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not in that binge sort of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating in front of other people?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve pretty much stopped meeting people for meals unless I absolutely have to. At work, if the guys are going out after a shift or someone suggests grabbing lunch, I’ll usually find a reason not to go, or I’ll say I’ve already eaten, or I’ve got something to finish. It’s not that I don’t want to see them. I just don’t want to be sitting there with people looking at what I ordered, or asking why I’m not eating much, or making comments like they’re joking but they’re still noticing.\n\nAnd it’s not only social. It’s starting to bleed into work in a way I don’t like talking about. Occupational health has held up my clearance for field-related duties, and I can tell the team is… they’re second-guessing whether I’m fit to be out there. Nobody says it outright in those words, but you can feel it. In my line of work, if people start wondering if you can hold up, that matters.\n\nSo yes, I avoid eating in front of people because I don’t want the questions, the pressure, the attention. I’d rather just not put myself in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered normal before restricting your intake?\n\nPatient: Before I started cutting back like this? Yes, I mean… I used to think of a normal amount as just a regular meal. Nothing dramatic. Breakfast, lunch, dinner if the day allowed it, or at least something you could sit down and call a meal.\n\nNow that feels different. What brings me in, really, is occupational health saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose if I’m honest, most days I keep it pretty minimal. Black coffee gets me through a lot of the day. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on maybe a few bites of chicken at night. And in my head that feels controlled, like I’m doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nA full meal now feels… I don’t know, like I’m letting my standards slip. Like I’ve given in somehow. I know that probably sounds strange out loud, because a normal person would just call it dinner, but to me it feels like too much. So yes, I did used to consider more food normal before I started restricting it, but now my sense of “normal” has shifted pretty far.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight related to the idea that you would lose control or become less disciplined if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, although the number matters to me more than I like admitting. If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this immediate jolt, like something’s gone wrong, like I’ve slipped. I know half a pound can be nothing, logically—water, whatever—but in the moment it doesn’t feel like nothing. It feels like proof that I’m losing control.\n\nAnd then I spend the rest of the day trying to correct it. I’ll cut back harder, be stricter, watch every little thing, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow even a small gain, then I’m becoming less disciplined, like I’m letting myself go in some way. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds extreme, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a large amount, no. That’s the thing—I don’t really have episodes where I sit down and eat a huge amount quickly, at least not the way I imagine you mean it.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve eaten more than I planned, and it feels like a lot to me because my idea of “a lot” is probably not very normal right now. Like, if I had something extra or more than I’d decided I was going to have, I might feel uncomfortable about it afterward. But an actual period where I’m eating a large amount of food in a short time… I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because what feels large in my head might not look large to someone else. But no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in a way that felt out of control?\n\nPatient: Out of control… I don’t think so, not in the way I imagine you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel uncomfortable if I eat more than I had planned, and then my head starts going over it and over it, like, “Why did you do that?” But that’s not the same as feeling like I couldn’t stop myself. It’s more that I’m very aware of every bite, if anything. I’m usually watching it too closely.\n\nThere might be moments where I feel I’ve gone off plan, and emotionally that can feel like I’ve messed up, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating in a way that’s out of control. I don’t have that sense of being carried along and unable to stop. If anything, it’s the opposite—I’m trying to keep everything very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in secret because you were ashamed of what or how much you were eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. I mean, I’m ashamed of eating sometimes, or ashamed if I feel like I ate more than I should have, but I don’t really hide away and eat secretly in the sense of sneaking food or waiting until no one’s around so I can eat a lot.\n\nIf anything, I tend to avoid eating in front of people because I feel watched or judged, but that’s a little different from what you’re asking, I think. It’s not like I’m secretly eating because I’m embarrassed by the amount. I’m usually more embarrassed that I’m eating at all, or that I might be doing it “wrong,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of times where I was eating in secret because of what or how much I was eating. I might be private about food, but not in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was over a period of time that might seem normal to others?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to answer that honestly, because “excessive” is a word I use in my own head a lot, but I’m not sure it would match what you mean clinically.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I felt like what I ate was excessive, but if you wrote it down on paper, I don’t know that another person would see it that way. Like if I had more than I’d planned, or something that wasn’t part of what I’d already decided was acceptable, then in my mind it can become “too much.” Even if it happened over a normal meal, or just across an evening, it can feel excessive to me because I’m measuring it against this very strict idea I have of what I should be eating.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had times where I objectively ate a huge amount over a normal period and thought, “That was excessive” in the way you might be asking. It’s more that my reaction to it feels disproportionate. I’ll think about it afterward and feel like I’ve overdone it, even if part of me knows someone else might say it was just… a meal, or a snack, or not especially unusual.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve considered things excessive, yes, but I’m not sure they actually were excessive by anyone else’s standards.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were binge eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it binge eating, no. I mean, I’ve worried about that word before, because if I eat more than I intended, my mind can make it feel like some terrible episode, like I’ve crossed a line. But when I try to look at it plainly, it doesn’t feel like what people usually mean by bingeing.\n\nI haven’t had that feeling of… sitting down and eating a large amount and feeling unable to stop. It’s more that I might have something I didn’t plan for, or more than I thought I “should,” and then I get very stuck on it afterward. I can label it harshly in my head, like I’ll think, “That was too much,” or “You lost control,” but if I’m being accurate, I don’t think I actually was out of control in the eating itself.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a binge-eating episode. I’ve had times where I felt really distressed about eating, or felt like I’d overeaten by my own standards, but not a true binge, at least not as I understand it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating where you felt like you couldn't stop once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you mean. I’ve had times where once I’ve eaten something, my thoughts sort of spiral afterward, and I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “That was too much,” even if it wasn’t actually much. But during the eating itself, I don’t usually feel like I physically or mentally can’t stop.\n\nIt’s more like I’m very aware of every bite, almost too aware, so it’s not this automatic thing where I start and then suddenly I’ve eaten a lot and couldn’t control it. If anything, I’m usually trying to stay in control the whole time, maybe too much. I might feel upset afterward if I went past whatever rule I had in my head, but I don’t remember episodes where I felt like, “I can’t stop eating now that I’ve started.”\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. I get distressed about eating, and I can judge myself pretty harshly, but I don’t think I have that can’t-stop-once-I-start feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating normally, without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Or… I guess there have been times where I might look normal from the outside, like if I’m sitting with someone and I have a coffee or I pick at something, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal at all. It feels managed. Controlled. Like I’m calculating the whole thing.\n\nMost days, if I’m being straight with you, I keep it very limited. Black coffee is easy because it feels clean, simple, like it doesn’t count in the same way. Maybe I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on at night a few bites of chicken, something basic. But a full meal… that’s hard for me. It feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m giving in to something I shouldn’t.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I’m here. Occupational health has been saying I’m not eating enough. I don’t always see it the same way in the moment, because to me it feels like discipline, or keeping myself where I need to be. But if you’re asking whether I have periods where I just eat normally without restricting it or thinking about it, no, I don’t think I really do. It’s almost always controlled in some way.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating primarily driven by a desire to maintain a certain body shape or appearance, or is it more about feeling in control of your food intake?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I think, but if I’m honest, the body shape part is much bigger than I like admitting. I tell myself it’s about control, and it is, in a way. Controlling food feels like controlling the day, controlling whether I’ve done well or failed. But the reason it matters so much is tied to how I look.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people say they see. If someone says I look thin, or that I don’t need to lose weight, it doesn’t land properly. I’ll still stand there and my eyes go straight to my stomach, my waist, that area. I’ll turn sideways, check whether it looks flat enough, lean enough. And then somehow that decides the whole day. If I think I look acceptable, I can respect myself a bit more. If I don’t, it’s like I’ve let myself go, even if nothing has actually changed.\n\nSo yes, the control is part of it, but it’s not just control in the abstract. It’s control aimed at keeping my body a certain way. Lean, contained, not soft. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s how it feels in my head. My food choices are very much driven by whether I think they’ll help me stay looking a certain way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I tell myself it’s about control, and it is, in a way. Controlling food feels like controlling the day, controlling whether I’ve done well or failed. But the reason it matters so much is tied to how I look.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people say they see. If someone says I look thin, or that I don’t need to lose weight, it doesn’t land properly. I’ll still stand there and my eyes go straight to my stomach, my waist, that area. I’ll turn sideways, check whether it looks flat enough, lean enough. And then somehow that decides the whole day. If I think I look acceptable, I can respect myself a bit more. If I don’t, it’s like I’ve let myself go, even if nothing has actually changed.\n\nSo yes, the control is part of it, but it’s not just control in the abstract. It’s control aimed at keeping my body a certain way. Lean, contained, not soft. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s how it feels in my head. My food choices are very much driven by whether I think they’ll help me stay looking a certain way.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or maintaining your current body shape?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more about avoiding weight gain, I think. Maintaining the shape is part of it, because that’s what I’m looking at and judging, but the thing that really hits me is the thought of the number going up.

If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I know logically that sounds tiny, and people would probably say it’s nothing, but it doesn’t feel like nothing to me. It feels like this jolt, like I’ve lost control of myself somehow. Like I’ve let something slip. And then the whole day changes around that. I’ll start cutting back harder, being stricter, almost like I have to correct it immediately.

It’s not just, “Oh, I’d prefer to stay the same.” It feels more urgent than that. I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on. Even a small increase makes me feel like if I don’t act on it, it’ll keep going, and I’ll lose the body I’ve been trying to keep. So yes… I’d say it’s mainly fear of gaining weight, and maintaining the shape is tied into that.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I’m here because occupational health pushed the issue. They’ve been saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose, if I’m being precise, most days it’s just black coffee for me. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or at night maybe a few bites of chicken, nothing elaborate.\n\nI don’t really see it the way they do, though. To me, sitting down to a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m losing discipline. I’ve always held myself to a certain line, and once I start making exceptions, it feels like the whole thing gets away from me. So that’s what brings me in, more or less.\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it as simply as “I want to be thinner.” It’s more that when I look in the mirror, I don’t see what other people apparently see. They’ll say I look too lean or whatever, but that’s not what my eye goes to.\n\nI look at my stomach, my waist, the line of it. I’ll turn sideways, check whether there’s any softness there, and honestly it can set the tone for the whole day. If I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself, like I’m still in control. If I don’t, it bothers me. It feels like I’ve let myself slip, even if someone else would say that doesn’t make sense.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I want to be leaner, or at least not any less lean than I am. But it’s not vanity, exactly. It feels more like a standard I’m trying to hold.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds irrational, or weak, but yes, I am.\n\nIf the scale is up even half a pound, I feel it immediately. It’s like this jolt in my chest, like I’ve lost control of the whole situation. I can know, logically, that half a pound could be anything, but that doesn’t really matter in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’ve let it start. You’re putting weight back on.” And once that thought is there, it’s very hard to shake.\n\nThen I’ll spend the rest of the day cutting back harder. I’ll tell myself I need to correct it, tighten things up, not give myself any room to slide. It’s not just that I dislike the number. I can’t stand the idea of gaining weight back, of becoming less lean. It feels like something I have to stop immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, because “excessive” is a bit subjective, isn’t it? I mean, compared with what other people eat, I don’t really know. Compared with what I think is appropriate, almost anything beyond what I planned can feel like too much.\n\nBut if you mean losing control and eating a large amount very quickly, I can’t really think of a clear example like that. I don’t tend to sit down and eat a lot all at once. If anything, I’m usually very aware of what I’m having, maybe too aware. Sometimes even a small amount can feel like I’ve overdone it, but I don’t know that it would look “excessive” to someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I’ve lost control while I’m eating, because I’m almost always paying attention to it—sometimes too closely. I’m aware of each bite, how much is left, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had enough. It’s not like I suddenly “come to” and realize I’ve eaten a lot without meaning to.\n\nThere are times where I feel unsettled if I eat more than I planned, or if something isn’t measured or structured the way I expected. Then I might feel like the situation is less controlled, if that makes sense. But that’s different from feeling unable to stop myself. It’s more that I feel anxious afterward, or like I need to mentally review what happened.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I lose control while eating. I tend to feel the opposite, really—like I’m trying very hard not to let anything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat until you feel uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… I’m trying to think of what you’d count as “uncomfortably full,” because I don’t really like feeling full at all. Even feeling normally full can make me uneasy, if I notice it too much.\n\nBut eating to the point where I’m physically stuffed, where my stomach hurts or I feel like I can’t move comfortably—no, that’s not really something I do. I tend to stop well before that, or at least I try to. If I feel any heaviness afterward, I’ll focus on it, maybe more than I should, and it can feel worse in my head than it probably is physically.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I eat until I’m uncomfortably full. It’s more that I’m uncomfortable with fullness in general, even when it’s not actually excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know you’ve asked it a couple of different ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult, it’s just… I wouldn’t describe what happens with me as eating a large amount in a short period.\n\nIf I eat more than I expected, it can feel like a big deal to me, but when I step back and think about the actual amount, it’s usually not what most people would call large. I don’t have episodes where I’m rapidly eating a lot of food or feeling like I can’t stop. I’m usually very aware of what I’m eating while it’s happening.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not in that binge sort of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating in front of other people?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve pretty much stopped meeting people for meals unless I absolutely have to. At work, if the guys are going out after a shift or someone suggests grabbing lunch, I’ll usually find a reason not to go, or I’ll say I’ve already eaten, or I’ve got something to finish. It’s not that I don’t want to see them. I just don’t want to be sitting there with people looking at what I ordered, or asking why I’m not eating much, or making comments like they’re joking but they’re still noticing.\n\nAnd it’s not only social. It’s starting to bleed into work in a way I don’t like talking about. Occupational health has held up my clearance for field-related duties, and I can tell the team is… they’re second-guessing whether I’m fit to be out there. Nobody says it outright in those words, but you can feel it. In my line of work, if people start wondering if you can hold up, that matters.\n\nSo yes, I avoid eating in front of people because I don’t want the questions, the pressure, the attention. I’d rather just not put myself in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered normal before restricting your intake?\n\nPatient: Before I started cutting back like this? Yes, I mean… I used to think of a normal amount as just a regular meal. Nothing dramatic. Breakfast, lunch, dinner if the day allowed it, or at least something you could sit down and call a meal.\n\nNow that feels different. What brings me in, really, is occupational health saying I’m not eating enough, and I suppose if I’m honest, most days I keep it pretty minimal. Black coffee gets me through a lot of the day. Sometimes I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on maybe a few bites of chicken at night. And in my head that feels controlled, like I’m doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nA full meal now feels… I don’t know, like I’m letting my standards slip. Like I’ve given in somehow. I know that probably sounds strange out loud, because a normal person would just call it dinner, but to me it feels like too much. So yes, I did used to consider more food normal before I started restricting it, but now my sense of “normal” has shifted pretty far.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight related to the idea that you would lose control or become less disciplined if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just the number itself, although the number matters to me more than I like admitting. If I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I feel this immediate jolt, like something’s gone wrong, like I’ve slipped. I know half a pound can be nothing, logically—water, whatever—but in the moment it doesn’t feel like nothing. It feels like proof that I’m losing control.\n\nAnd then I spend the rest of the day trying to correct it. I’ll cut back harder, be stricter, watch every little thing, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow even a small gain, then I’m becoming less disciplined, like I’m letting myself go in some way. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds extreme, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a large amount, no. That’s the thing—I don’t really have episodes where I sit down and eat a huge amount quickly, at least not the way I imagine you mean it.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve eaten more than I planned, and it feels like a lot to me because my idea of “a lot” is probably not very normal right now. Like, if I had something extra or more than I’d decided I was going to have, I might feel uncomfortable about it afterward. But an actual period where I’m eating a large amount of food in a short time… I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because what feels large in my head might not look large to someone else. But no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in a way that felt out of control?\n\nPatient: Out of control… I don’t think so, not in the way I imagine you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel uncomfortable if I eat more than I had planned, and then my head starts going over it and over it, like, “Why did you do that?” But that’s not the same as feeling like I couldn’t stop myself. It’s more that I’m very aware of every bite, if anything. I’m usually watching it too closely.\n\nThere might be moments where I feel I’ve gone off plan, and emotionally that can feel like I’ve messed up, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating in a way that’s out of control. I don’t have that sense of being carried along and unable to stop. If anything, it’s the opposite—I’m trying to keep everything very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating in secret because you were ashamed of what or how much you were eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly. I mean, I’m ashamed of eating sometimes, or ashamed if I feel like I ate more than I should have, but I don’t really hide away and eat secretly in the sense of sneaking food or waiting until no one’s around so I can eat a lot.\n\nIf anything, I tend to avoid eating in front of people because I feel watched or judged, but that’s a little different from what you’re asking, I think. It’s not like I’m secretly eating because I’m embarrassed by the amount. I’m usually more embarrassed that I’m eating at all, or that I might be doing it “wrong,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of times where I was eating in secret because of what or how much I was eating. I might be private about food, but not in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was over a period of time that might seem normal to others?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to answer that honestly, because “excessive” is a word I use in my own head a lot, but I’m not sure it would match what you mean clinically.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I felt like what I ate was excessive, but if you wrote it down on paper, I don’t know that another person would see it that way. Like if I had more than I’d planned, or something that wasn’t part of what I’d already decided was acceptable, then in my mind it can become “too much.” Even if it happened over a normal meal, or just across an evening, it can feel excessive to me because I’m measuring it against this very strict idea I have of what I should be eating.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had times where I objectively ate a huge amount over a normal period and thought, “That was excessive” in the way you might be asking. It’s more that my reaction to it feels disproportionate. I’ll think about it afterward and feel like I’ve overdone it, even if part of me knows someone else might say it was just… a meal, or a snack, or not especially unusual.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve considered things excessive, yes, but I’m not sure they actually were excessive by anyone else’s standards.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were binge eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it binge eating, no. I mean, I’ve worried about that word before, because if I eat more than I intended, my mind can make it feel like some terrible episode, like I’ve crossed a line. But when I try to look at it plainly, it doesn’t feel like what people usually mean by bingeing.\n\nI haven’t had that feeling of… sitting down and eating a large amount and feeling unable to stop. It’s more that I might have something I didn’t plan for, or more than I thought I “should,” and then I get very stuck on it afterward. I can label it harshly in my head, like I’ll think, “That was too much,” or “You lost control,” but if I’m being accurate, I don’t think I actually was out of control in the eating itself.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a binge-eating episode. I’ve had times where I felt really distressed about eating, or felt like I’d overeaten by my own standards, but not a true binge, at least not as I understand it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating where you felt like you couldn't stop once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you mean. I’ve had times where once I’ve eaten something, my thoughts sort of spiral afterward, and I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “That was too much,” even if it wasn’t actually much. But during the eating itself, I don’t usually feel like I physically or mentally can’t stop.\n\nIt’s more like I’m very aware of every bite, almost too aware, so it’s not this automatic thing where I start and then suddenly I’ve eaten a lot and couldn’t control it. If anything, I’m usually trying to stay in control the whole time, maybe too much. I might feel upset afterward if I went past whatever rule I had in my head, but I don’t remember episodes where I felt like, “I can’t stop eating now that I’ve started.”\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. I get distressed about eating, and I can judge myself pretty harshly, but I don’t think I have that can’t-stop-once-I-start feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like you were eating normally, without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Or… I guess there have been times where I might look normal from the outside, like if I’m sitting with someone and I have a coffee or I pick at something, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal at all. It feels managed. Controlled. Like I’m calculating the whole thing.\n\nMost days, if I’m being straight with you, I keep it very limited. Black coffee is easy because it feels clean, simple, like it doesn’t count in the same way. Maybe I’ll have a plain yogurt, or later on at night a few bites of chicken, something basic. But a full meal… that’s hard for me. It feels like I’m letting my standards slip, like I’m giving in to something I shouldn’t.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I’m here. Occupational health has been saying I’m not eating enough. I don’t always see it the same way in the moment, because to me it feels like discipline, or keeping myself where I need to be. But if you’re asking whether I have periods where I just eat normally without restricting it or thinking about it, no, I don’t think I really do. It’s almost always controlled in some way.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating primarily driven by a desire to maintain a certain body shape or appearance, or is it more about feeling in control of your food intake?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I think, but if I’m honest, the body shape part is much bigger than I like admitting. I tell myself it’s about control, and it is, in a way. Controlling food feels like controlling the day, controlling whether I’ve done well or failed. But the reason it matters so much is tied to how I look.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people say they see. If someone says I look thin, or that I don’t need to lose weight, it doesn’t land properly. I’ll still stand there and my eyes go straight to my stomach, my waist, that area. I’ll turn sideways, check whether it looks flat enough, lean enough. And then somehow that decides the whole day. If I think I look acceptable, I can respect myself a bit more. If I don’t, it’s like I’ve let myself go, even if nothing has actually changed.\n\nSo yes, the control is part of it, but it’s not just control in the abstract. It’s control aimed at keeping my body a certain way. Lean, contained, not soft. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s how it feels in my head. My food choices are very much driven by whether I think they’ll help me stay looking a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or maintaining your current body shape?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding weight gain, I think. Maintaining the shape is part of it, because that’s what I’m looking at and judging, but the thing that really hits me is the thought of the number going up.\n\nIf I get on the scale and it’s up even half a pound, I know logically that sounds tiny, and people would probably say it’s nothing, but it doesn’t feel like nothing to me. It feels like this jolt, like I’ve lost control of myself somehow. Like I’ve let something slip. And then the whole day changes around that. I’ll start cutting back harder, being stricter, almost like I have to correct it immediately.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I’d prefer to stay the same.” It feels more urgent than that. I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on. Even a small increase makes me feel like if I don’t act on it, it’ll keep going, and I’ll lose the body I’ve been trying to keep. So yes… I’d say it’s mainly fear of gaining weight, and maintaining the shape is tied into that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
